Contraceptive calendar analysis

Modern-method discontinuation

A single home for the Ethiopia discontinuation analyses. New results can be appended as additional sections while retaining the same definitions, methods, and validation record.

Individual women’s IR fileCalendar months 3–62 before interviewWeighted life tablesUpdated 27 September 2026
Method groups. Rows are injectables, implants, pill, emergency contraception, Other modern methods, and their requested aggregate. Other modern methods includes IUD, male condom, female sterilization, male sterilization, Lactational Amenorrhea Method (LAM), Standard Days Method (SDM), and other modern method.

EC-inclusive initiation diagnostic

This earlier diagnostic retains EC to document the original DHS method coding. The primary EC-excluded initiation mix is in the Presentation figures section below.

This analysis describes the methods women started during months 3–62 before interview. It includes 7,859 unweighted observed episode starts contributed by 5,704 women, corresponding to 9,185.5 weighted starts. All reported initiation shares and charts use the weighted denominator. It excludes delayed-entry discontinuation episodes because those methods began before the initiation observation window.

How to read this: initiation is a flow, not a stock. The raw sample contains 7,859 starts, but the distribution uses 9,185.5 weighted starts. A method with many starts creates a larger cohort exposed to its subsequent discontinuation profile. Women may contribute more than one start.

Weighting and complex survey design

Point estimates use the DHS individual woman’s weight v005/1,000,000 for every initiation episode contributed by that woman. Standard errors and 95% confidence intervals use Taylor linearization with DHS sampling strata v022 and primary sampling units v021. Because all episode starts from one woman remain within her PSU, the PSU-clustered calculation retains their dependence rather than treating starts as independent simple-random-sample observations.

Other modern methods includes IUD, male condom, female sterilization, male sterilization, Lactational Amenorrhea Method (LAM), Standard Days Method (SDM), and other modern method.

DHS life-table scope and late entry. The report-style discontinuation tables use contraceptive episodes observed 3–62 months before interview. Episodes that began before this 60-month observation window but whose start is recorded in the calendar join the risk set only after their already-completed months of use; episodes ongoing when the calendar itself begins are excluded because their starts are unknown. The reproduced Table 7.11 has 9,068 unweighted episodes across all contraceptive methods (10,467 weighted), including 1,008 late entries. Its month-one risk set has 8,060 unweighted episodes (9,406 weighted), so the printed weighted episode count is not the denominator of every monthly hazard. Subsequent modern-method analyses exclude emergency contraception and traditional methods but retain eligible late entries in their duration-specific risk sets. Initiation-mix figures intentionally count only observed starts and answer a different question. .

The aggregate contains all modern methods represented in these rows and excludes traditional methods. It is therefore distinct from the published all-methods rate of 39.8%.

How revised Table 7.11 was calculated

This table uses the standard DHS weighted multiple-decrement life-table method. Each continuous period of using one method is an episode. During a duration month, an episode may continue, discontinue for one of nine mutually exclusive reasons, or be censored because observation ended while use continued.

For each month and reason, the weighted discontinuations are divided by the weighted episodes still at risk. The reason-specific probabilities are accumulated across months using survival from all discontinuation causes. The nine reason-specific cumulative incidences therefore add up to the Any reason rate:

Any-reason discontinuation at time t = 1 − S(t) = sum of the reason-specific cumulative incidences

The reasons are competing outcomes: once an episode ends for one reason, it cannot later end for another reason. “Switched to another method” is estimated using the same life-table survival probabilities, but is a subset of discontinuation rather than an additional reason. The directly comparable method rows reproduce the published DHS Table 7.11 values at displayed precision.

Discontinuation by time

Cumulative percentage of episodes discontinued for any reason by 3, 6, 9, and 12 months.

Important: “Any reason” means the original method episode ended. It includes women who immediately switched to another modern method, so these rates are method-episode discontinuation rates and should not be interpreted as exit from all modern-method use.

Each cell is the cumulative probability that an episode ends for that reason within 12 months of starting the method. The nine stated reasons are mutually exclusive and, before rounding, sum to Any reason. These are risks per 100 method episodes, not percentages among episodes that have already discontinued.

This reproduces Ethiopia DHS 2024–25, Table 7.11 (printed p. 140). The report’s “Other” row includes modern and traditional methods; “All methods” also includes traditional methods and emergency contraception. Parentheses follow the report’s caution for emergency contraception. “Switched” overlaps with the nine reasons and is not added to them. All 66 displayed rate cells and six weighted episode counts match the report at its printed precision.

How each reason-specific value is calculated

For each method and month of use, we divide the weighted episodes that end for a given reason by the weighted episodes still at risk in that month. We multiply that monthly reason-specific probability by the probability the original method survived all reasons through the previous month, then add the contributions through month 12. Episodes still in use at the end of observed calendar time are censored; episodes that first enter the observation window after initiation enter the risk set at their observed duration.

Reason-specific risk by month 12 = Σm=1…12 Sall(m−1) × [weighted endings for that reason in month m ÷ weighted episodes at risk in month m]

The survival term is updated with endings for every reason. This is the DHS multiple-decrement life-table calculation. It is a cumulative incidence, not a risk conditional on avoiding other reasons.

Worked example: injectable episodes

By month 12, 44.5% have ended injectable use for any reason. Excluding “desire to become pregnant” (18.379%) and “husband/partner disapproved” (0.554%) leaves 25.5% of episodes (44.471 − 18.379 − 0.554 = 25.539%). Also excluding method failure (0.841%) and other fertility-related reasons (4.142%) leaves 20.6% (20.556% before rounding). Excluding the residual “Other reasons” (2.631%) as well leaves 17.9% (17.925% before rounding). These are sums of the retained reason-specific cumulative probabilities, not new conditional life tables.

12-month discontinuation excluding wanted pregnancy and partner disapproval

This is the weighted competing-risks cumulative probability that the original method episode ends within 12 months for any recorded reason other than “desire to become pregnant” or “husband/partner disapproved.” All eligible episodes remain in the initial risk set. Endings for the two excluded reasons still remove episodes from later monthly risk sets; they are not treated as censoring.

Definition: This column sums the 12-month cumulative incidences for all stated discontinuation reasons except desire to become pregnant and husband/partner disapproved, using the same underlying life table as the all-cause rate. It still includes method failure, other fertility-related reasons, side effects or health concerns, wanting a more effective method, access or cost, and residual other or unknown reasons. “Other modern methods” excludes IUD and emergency contraception; “All modern methods” includes IUD but excludes emergency contraception and traditional methods. These are original-method episode endings, including some switches, not necessarily exits from modern contraception.

Values come from the same weighted, 12-month competing-risks life table as the discontinuation comparison above. They are shown to two decimal places so the small components remain visible; subtotals can differ from displayed row sums by 0.01 because of rounding. The smallest component, difficulty becoming pregnant or menopause, has only eight observed first-year endings and should be interpreted cautiously.

How the DHS switching column fits

Switching describes what happened after the original method ended; it is not a mutually exclusive stated reason. DHS counts a switch when a different method is used in the following month, or when the reason is “wanted a more effective method” and a different method starts within two months. Its 12-month probability is calculated with the same weighted monthly risk sets and all-cause survival term. It can overlap with several reason columns and should not be added to Any reason.

For example, the injectable switching probability is 5.1% of injectable episodes, while pill switching is 15.7% of pill episodes. Switching to a traditional method counts in DHS’s switching column. To measure losses from the mCPR numerator, we must separately distinguish modern-to-modern switches from moves to traditional methods or no method.

Direct modern-to-modern switching (custom calculation, not the published DHS switching column): Among eligible modern-method episodes excluding emergency contraception, the 12-month life-table probability of ending the original method and using a different modern method in the immediately following month is 5.16% (95% stratified PSU-bootstrap interval: 4.4–6.1%). The any-reason ending probability for the same episodes is 39.088%; therefore, 5.160 ÷ 39.088 = 13.2% of first-year method endings on the life-table scale. The 5.16% denominator is all eligible episodes, whereas the 13.2% denominator is episodes estimated to end within 12 months. The broader DHS switching measure also allows traditional-method destinations and its specified one-month-gap exception. A modern-to-modern switch ends the original method but does not itself remove a woman from modern use.

The weighted episode count describes the DHS-comparable discontinuation cohort. It contains 8,850 raw modern-method episodes (10,231.3 weighted), including 991 delayed-entry episodes (1,045.8 weighted) that began before month 62 but contribute exposure during the life-table window. It is therefore larger than the initiation cohort of 7,859 raw starts (9,185.5 weighted). The life table’s risk set changes at each month as episodes discontinue or are censored.

How the columns relate: At 12 months, “Ended without successful modern switch” plus “Successful modern switch” equals “Total original-method discontinuation.” The published DHS Any reason rate is the total and therefore includes successful switchers; for example, 39.6% + 4.8% = 44.5% for injectables and 52.0% + 15.5% = 67.4% for pill, allowing for rounding.

Switching definition: A successful modern switch means a different specified modern method was used in the month after the original method ended, or—when “wanted a more effective method” was the stated reason—after one intervening month without use. This applies the DHS Table 7.11 timing rule while restricting the destination to a modern method. “Ended without successful modern switch” is useful for distinguishing immediate retention in modern use, but it is not a net 12-month loss measure because a woman may re-enter modern-method use later. Other modern methods includes IUD, male condom, female sterilization, male sterilization, LAM, SDM, and other modern method.

Cause-specific cumulative discontinuation at 3, 6, and 12 months

Each cell is the cumulative probability that an eligible method episode had ended for the stated reason by the indicated month. These are probabilities among all eligible episodes, rather than the distribution of reasons only among episodes that discontinued.

Color interpretation: Fertility-related combines desire to become pregnant and other fertility-related reasons. Side effects / bleeding combines changes in menstrual bleeding and other side effects or health concerns. Method, access, or effectiveness combines method failure, wanting a more effective method, and access, cost, or inconvenience. Partner disapproval and Other reasons remain separate. The detailed tables below retain all nine reason categories separately.

The 12-month table reproduces the reason-specific results in revised Table 7.11.

How the time-point discontinuation rates were calculated

All reasons for stopping are combined into one event. This produces a weighted single-decrement life table, numerically equivalent to a weighted Kaplan–Meier survival analysis. At each duration month, S(t) is the estimated probability of continuing the method and 1 − S(t) is the cumulative probability of having discontinued by that month. The calculation accounts for episodes that remain in use when observation ends.

How to interpret the injectable row

  • 3 months — 16.2%: An estimated 16.2% of injectable-use episodes had discontinued by the end of month 3; approximately 83.8% were continuing.
  • 6 months — 25.6%: An estimated 25.6% had discontinued by the end of month 6; approximately 74.4% were continuing.
  • 9 months — 34.7%: An estimated 34.7% had discontinued by the end of month 9; approximately 65.3% were continuing.
  • 12 months — 44.5%: An estimated 44.5% had discontinued by the end of month 12; approximately 55.5% were continuing.

These estimates are cumulative. The 25.6% at six months is contained within the 44.5% at 12 months. The increase between six and 12 months is 18.9 percentage points (44.5 − 25.6), meaning that an additional 18.9% of the original episode cohort discontinued during months 7–12.

Approximately 57.5% of all discontinuation accumulated by 12 months had already occurred by six months (25.6 ÷ 44.5). Among episodes still continuing at six months, the estimated probability of discontinuing before month 12 is approximately 25.4%:

(44.5 − 25.6) ÷ (100 − 25.6) = 25.4%

The 18.9-point difference is a cumulative increase on the original episode-cohort scale. It is not a monthly hazard and should not be described as 18.9% of the women still using injectables at six months.

Revised Table 7.12: reasons for discontinuation

Weighted percent distribution of modern-method discontinuations in the five years preceding the survey, by the main reason recorded for ending the method episode.

Aggregated reason categories

The 15 detailed reasons are combined below into the same nine broad categories used in the discontinuation life-table analysis. Reasons are ranked by their weighted share across all specified modern methods; “most important” here means most frequently reported.

How to read the overall reasons chart: Its denominator is the modern-method episodes observed to discontinue in the five-year calendar: 6,409 unweighted endings, or 7,326.2 after survey weighting, excluding emergency contraception. For example, 47.6% means that 47.6% of these endings were recorded as wanted pregnancy. It is a distribution of stated reasons, not the probability that a method user will discontinue.

Reasons for discontinuation differ by method

Among all observed modern-method episodes that ended in the five-year calendar, each bar shows the weighted distribution of recorded reasons for ending that method. The percentages sum to 100 within each method. This is a distribution among discontinued episodes, not a 12-month discontinuation probability.

How to read the bars by method: Each bar divides the observed discontinuations of that method into stated reasons, so it sums to 100%. For example, among injectable episodes that ended, 50.4% were recorded as wanted pregnancy and 35.9% as method experience or access. The all-modern bar uses all 7,326.2 weighted observed modern-method endings. These shares do not give the risk of ending injectable use—or any other method—within 12 months.

Original figure categories: Method experience/access combines method failure, wanted a more effective method, bleeding changes, other side effects or health concerns, and access, cost, or inconvenience. Other reasons includes the three other fertility-related reasons, fatalistic, unspecified Other, and don’t know.

This companion figure excludes only episodes recorded as ending because the woman wanted to become pregnant or her husband/partner disapproved. Each bar sums to 100% among the retained discontinued episodes of that method.

How to interpret this table

This is a distribution among episodes that discontinued. It answers, “Of the discontinuations for this method, what percentage occurred for each stated reason?” It does not show the probability that a method will be discontinued; those probabilities are reported in the life-table sections. The table displays all 15 reason categories printed in Ethiopia DHS Table 7.12; the interpretation below highlights only the five largest aggregate categories.

Across the specified modern methods, wanting to become pregnant is the largest stated reason (46.7%), followed by wanting a more effective method (13.7%), infrequent sex or husband away (7.9%), other side effects or health concerns (7.0%), and inconvenience (6.6%). The reason profile differs markedly by method—for emergency contraception, infrequent sex or husband away accounts for 56.0% of recorded discontinuations.

Calculation and validation

Following the DHS Table 7.12 tabulation, every nonblank discontinuation reason in calendar positions v018 through v018+59 is paired with the method recorded in the same month. Percentages use v005/1,000,000. Design-based standard errors and confidence intervals, available in the detailed results, use strata v022 and PSUs v021.

The released microdata reproduce all 135 published method-specific percentages and 149 of all 150 percentage cells at one decimal place. The single difference is all-method “lack of access/too far” (2.5% in the released data versus 2.4% printed). The report prints 4,689 weighted injectable discontinuations and 7,656 overall; the released file yields 4,682.0 and 7,648.9. These small count differences do not affect the matching method-specific percentages.

What happens after discontinuation?

These figures describe the subsequent calendar states of modern-method episodes that discontinued for any recorded reason, including pregnancy-related reasons. Both use the same cohort of 5,118 observed discontinuation events, representing 5,831.0 weighted events, with all 12 subsequent calendar months available.

How to read the denominators: everyone in these figures has already discontinued a modern-method episode. The figures describe what happened afterward; they do not estimate the probability that an episode will discontinue. The percentages in the alluvial use all matched discontinuations as the denominator. The percentages within each stacked bar use discontinuations of that originating method as the denominator.

State one month after discontinuation

Example: Injectables (63.5%) means that 63.5% of the matched discontinuation events originated with injectables. No method (75.5%) means that 75.5% of all matched discontinuation events had no contraceptive method recorded one month later.

State 12 months after discontinuation

How to interpret the 12-month bars

Among matched injectable discontinuations, 24.8% were using a modern method at month 12, 50.2% had no method recorded, 24.5% were in a pregnancy/birth/termination state, and 0.5% were using a traditional method. These are month-12 snapshots. They do not show whether a woman changed states during the intervening months.

The complete-follow-up restriction excludes 1,430 observed discontinuation events (1,670.7 weighted) occurring too recently to observe month 12. The full Table 7.12-style modern-method cohort contains 6,548 events (7,501.7 weighted); the matched figures use the 5,118-event subset so the month-1 and month-12 views are directly comparable.

Colors match the presentation palette, with neutral gray for no method. Every category is also directly labeled or identified in a legend so the figures remain interpretable without color.

Exploratory alluvials: discontinuations for reasons other than pregnancy

These figures exclude the recorded reasons “became pregnant while using” and “wanted to become pregnant.” They retain 2,499 observed discontinuations (2,688.5 weighted events) with 12 months of follow-up. The filter applies to the stated reason for stopping; pregnancy, birth, or termination can still be a later calendar state. Every percentage uses these matched non-pregnancy-reason events as its denominator.

From episode evidence to program action

This section combines the timing, stated reason, and post-discontinuation destination analyses. The first two figures remain conditional on an episode having discontinued; the historical current-user pathway uses women as the denominator.

What programs can do

Observed signalEthiopia 2024–25 findingPotential program response
Injectable discontinuation is already visible early16.2% cumulative episode discontinuation by month 3; 44.5% by month 12Prioritize follow-up, counseling, and resupply during the first three months
Bleeding, side effects, or health concerns12.9% of injectable discontinuations; 76.3% had no method next monthProvide anticipatory counseling, side-effect management, and immediate alternatives
Wanted a more effective method11.5% of injectable discontinuations; 47.9% used a modern method next monthMake same-visit switching easy and preserve uninterrupted modern use
Access, cost, or inconvenience9.4% of injectable discontinuations; 69.7% had no method next monthStrengthen commodity availability, reminders, outreach, and convenient reinjection
Pregnancy intention50.4% of injectable discontinuationsDistinguish intended fertility exits and connect women to preconception or later postpartum support
No method immediately after an episode ends75.5% of matched modern-method discontinuations had no method recorded one month laterPrevent avoidable gaps through rapid switching; use re-entry outreach when a gap occurs

Program responses are hypotheses motivated by the combined evidence; the descriptive calendar analysis does not establish that a specific service failure caused an individual discontinuation.

Injectables: stated reason and state one month later

Injectables: timing and immediate destination heat map

Interpretation boundary: duration cells summarize completed discontinued episodes and are not life-table risks. Use the validated life table to report the probability that an episode ends by month 3 or month 12.

Historical current-user pathway: baseline to months 3 and 12

What this pathway adds

Unlike the episode alluvial, this denominator includes continuing users. It therefore estimates the weighted distribution of continuation, uninterrupted switching, gross modern-use exits, re-entry, and endpoint absence among baseline current users. It is a historical landmark prototype; method- and duration-standardized probabilities are the next modeling step before applying results to the interview-date current-user stock.

Baseline: one historical landmark per woman at month 15 before interview. Follow-up: the subsequent 12 months, ending at month 3 before interview. Successful switching: uninterrupted modern-to-modern use, plus the DHS one-month-gap convention when the stated reason is wanting a more effective method and a different modern method follows.

Standardized current-user stock-to-outcome pathway

This is the principal current-user estimand. Historical 12-month pathway probabilities are estimated within method-duration strata and standardized to the weighted method-duration distribution of women using a modern method at the survey interview.

How to interpret the standardized pathway

28.1% are expected to experience at least one gross modern-use exit during the next 12 months. Of all current users, 4.7% are expected to re-enter modern use by month 12, while 23.4% are expected to remain absent from modern use at the endpoint. The complementary 71.9% are expected to remain continuously in modern use, including 4.7% who switch methods without a qualifying exit.

Standardization: historical cells use four method groups and five baseline-duration groups (0–3, 4–6, 7–12, 13–24, and 25+ months). The current-stock weights come from v312, v337, and v005. The smallest historical cell is pill users at 4–6 months (18 women), so cell-specific uncertainty and a pooled-duration sensitivity analysis are required before final external reporting.

Methods

Episode construction

Continuous calendar runs of one contraceptive method define episodes. The analysis covers months 3–62 before interview. Starts already underway at the calendar’s beginning are excluded from initiation counts. The discontinuation life table can retain eligible ongoing episodes with delayed entry at their observed method duration.

Weights and censoring

Episodes use the woman’s individual sample weight, v005/1,000,000. Episodes still in use at the end of observation are right-censored and contribute exposure through their last observed month.

Reason-specific table

Table 7.11 uses a multiple-decrement life table. The nine mutually exclusive reasons are competing event types, and their cumulative incidences sum to any-reason discontinuation.

Any-reason table

When all reasons are combined as one event, the cumulative estimate is equivalent to a single-decrement weighted Kaplan–Meier survival analysis. Rates are reported at duration months 3, 6, 9, and 12.

Survey design and uncertainty

Point estimates use v005/1,000,000. Inference retains all 797 PSUs represented in the IR file across 27 v022 strata, including 85 PSUs with no eligible complete-case modern-method episodes. Repeated episodes and months from a woman remain in her PSU. Counts labeled N are unweighted sample sizes; method shares, reason shares and alluvial flows are weighted descriptive estimates.

Regional and subgroup adjusted intervals use stratified PSU bootstrap refits (500 for the principal regional models; 300 for the subgroup model variants). Coefficient tests use a full-frame stratified PSU sandwich covariance and 770 design degrees of freedom. The early all-cause subgroup view is reconstructed with a weighted monthly logistic model adjusting for month of use, method, age, parity, education, wealth, residence and region; its probabilities average over eligible episode profiles, with full-frame linearized intervals. Regional all-cause life-table and reason-share intervals also use full-frame linearization.

Planning intervals use 500 positive-multiplier PSU bootstrap replicates within strata, re-estimating current-use composition, historical probabilities, AFT parameters and switching pathways. Within a stratum with n PSUs, normalized Gamma multipliers of shape (n−2)/n have mean 1, variance 1 and covariance −1/(n−1), matching the first two moments of rescaled Rao–Wu PSU weights while retaining sparse landmark denominators. AFT distribution families, population and growth assumptions remain fixed. The original AFT cohort includes visible episode starts; DHS-style raw life tables retain eligible delayed-entry episodes. Cross-validated risk-band intervals condition on fitted bands and omit training uncertainty.

Pairwise tests use the covariance of regional differences from the same replicate. Holm correction applies within the stated comparison family; marginal 95% intervals are pointwise unless explicitly labeled familywise. One of 10,000 raw all-region draws had an empty risk set; paired tests retain the 9,999 complete draws. At 5%, the main all-modern observed, common-reference Model 1 and fully adjusted Model 2 each distinguish Tigray–Amhara and Tigray–Addis Ababa. Pair-specific shared method mixes additionally distinguish Central Ethiopia–Amhara. For the injectable-only fully adjusted model, only Tigray–Amhara meets 5% correction; South West Ethiopia and Sidama comparisons with Tigray meet 10% and remain exploratory.

Validation

Published-table check passed. Injectables, implants, pill, and emergency contraception match every corresponding published Table 7.11 cell at the displayed precision. At 12 months, the any-reason rates are 44.5%, 16.0%, 67.4%, and 87.2%, respectively.

An independent weighted Kaplan–Meier calculation matches the any-reason estimates at all four time points to numerical precision. The regrouped Other modern methods row and All specified modern methods row are new constructs and do not have direct comparison rows in the DHS report.

Additional analyses

Future analyses will be added here as new sections, keeping this page as the project’s consolidated analysis record.

Presentation figures

Primary scope: Emergency contraception (EC) is excluded from the initiation-mix and 12-month method-discontinuation figures below. The DHS validation tables retain EC for published-report comparability. The separate post-discontinuation pathway and official-mCPR current-user scenarios retain DHS modern-method coding, including EC, and have not been recalculated under this narrower definition.

Entry methods set the denominator for future retention risk

Initiation mix among modern-method starts

These figures use 7,699 observed modern-method starts excluding EC (8,993.7 weighted). Each method share is out of modern starts other than EC; the overall and annual figures above also include traditional-method starts in their denominator.

Timing of modern-method discontinuation

The step curves show the weighted life-table probability of ending the original method by each completed month. The teal labels mark injectable discontinuation at months 3 (16.2%) and 6 (25.6%); direct labels show every method at month 12. Method switching counts as an ending of the original method. EC is excluded throughout. “Other modern” excludes both EC and IUD and includes male condom, female and male sterilization, LAM, SDM, and other modern methods.

Timing of discontinuation excluding wanted pregnancy and partner disapproval

This companion curve uses the same original-method episodes, survey weights, monthly risk sets, and all-cause survival as the curve above. It counts endings for every recorded reason except “desire to become pregnant” and “husband/partner disapproved.” Those excluded endings remain competing events; they are not censored or removed from the initial denominator. Method failure, other fertility-related reasons, and residual other reasons are included.

How much occurs in the first 3 and 6 months?

The values below are cumulative probabilities using the same reason exclusions as the curve. Comparing 3 months with 12 months shows how much of the first-year discontinuation occurs early; the columns are not separate interval rates.

Method starts and 12-month discontinuation

Method starts and 12-month discontinuation excluding wanted pregnancy and partner disapproval

This companion figure keeps the EC-excluded method-start shares from the chart above. Its dark-blue bars replace the all-reason discontinuation probabilities with the 12-month cumulative probability of ending the original method for any reason except wanting to become pregnant or husband/partner disapproval. These endpoints match the corrected curve and the second column of the comparison table below.

Each amber share has the same denominator: all 9,214.3 weighted observed contraceptive method starts other than EC, including modern and traditional methods. A start can be a first use, restart, or switch to a method; ongoing episodes that started before the observation window are not counted as starts. The blue probability instead uses eligible original-method episodes for that method. These percentages answer different questions and should not be subtracted from one another. Switching methods still counts as ending the original method; the blue bar is not a modern-use exit rate. “Other modern methods” excludes IUD and EC.

12-month discontinuation probability by method and reason

How to read the 12-month figure: For each method, the denominator begins with all eligible episodes of that original method, whether they ended, continued, or were censored. The life table updates the weighted at-risk set each month. A 44.5% injectable total means an estimated 44.5% probability of ending the original injectable episode for any reason within 12 months; its 18.4-point wanted-pregnancy and 18.8-point method-experience segments are cause-specific contributions to that total. For the all-modern row, 8,682 eligible episodes (10,035.7 weighted), excluding emergency contraception, produce a 39.1% all-reason 12-month probability. The DHS report's separate all-methods row includes traditional methods and emergency contraception and reports 39.8%; that is not the row plotted here. Ending the original method may include switching to another method.

Why the leading reason changes between figures: In the five-year distribution among observed modern-method endings, wanted pregnancy accounts for 47.6% and method experience or access for 38.4%. In the first-year life table among all eligible modern-method episodes, the respective cumulative probabilities are 14.9% and 17.0%. The denominator and observation period differ, so these results do not conflict. The figures alone do not establish the timing of each stated reason; that requires a cause-specific timing analysis.

What contributes to 12-month discontinuation from method experience or access?

This companion to the all-reason 12-month figure splits its method experience or access segment into the four reason groups used in the preceding reason-distribution drill-down. Each stacked bar uses all eligible episodes of the original method as its life-table base. Its segments are 12-month probability contributions in percentage points and add to the method experience or access total printed at right. They do not add to 100%.

Within the method experience or access total

For all modern methods combined, the 12-month probability is 17.0%. The table gives the share of that method-specific total due to each reason, so its four reason columns sum to 100% within each row.

12-month discontinuation excluding wanted pregnancy and partner disapproval

This companion graph excludes the same two stated reasons as the restricted 100% reason-distribution chart: wanted pregnancy and husband/partner disapproval. Reading the figure: Blue combines method failure, bleeding changes, side effects or health concerns, wanting a more effective method, and access, cost or inconvenience. Gray combines other fertility-related and residual other or unknown reasons. A method ending can include a switch to another method, so these bars do not measure exits from modern contraception.

DHS Table 7.11: 12-month discontinuation by method and reason

Scope: The DHS “All methods” row includes traditional methods (10,467 weighted episodes). Its “Other methods” row combines modern and traditional methods not shown separately. Our presentation’s all-modern total excluding emergency contraception instead uses 10,035.7 weighted eligible episodes and has a 39.1% all-reason probability. Why this N differs from the reason-distribution figure: That figure uses 7,326.2 weighted modern-method episodes, excluding EC, that were observed to discontinue within the five-year calendar. Table 7.11 includes continuing and censored episodes and reports probabilities of ending within the first 12 months of method use. “Switched” is a subset of any-reason discontinuation and is not added to the reason columns. Parentheses around EC values reproduce the report’s small-sample caution.

From reasons to risk

Reasons describe why observed method episodes ended. The next question is how the 12-month probability of ending for the selected reasons varies across groups of eligible episodes.

Earlier specification: the regression figure below uses 0, 1–2, and 3+ parity and does not include marital status. The updated full model in the subgroup section uses 0, 1–2, 3–4, and 5+ parity and four marital-status groups, with new intervals. Do not transfer the earlier confidence intervals to the revised estimates.

Who is more likely to discontinue for reasons other than wanted pregnancy or partner disapproval?

This regression mirrors the selected-reason 12-month life table. Its denominator is all eligible modern-method episodes, including episodes that continue. The event is ending the original method within 12 months for any recorded reason except wanting to become pregnant or husband/partner disapproval. Those two excluded endings are competing events: they remain in the starting group but do not count toward the selected-reason probability. Method failure, other fertility-related reasons, and residual Other reasons remain included. A switch still counts as ending the original method.

Unadjusted and adjusted 12-month probabilities by group

Both columns use the same complete-case set of eligible episodes. The unadjusted column is a weighted competing-risks life table calculated separately within each group. The adjusted column compares groups after accounting for original method, duration, the other characteristics below, region, and start year; it is standardized to a common distribution of observed method starts.

Direct comparisons: The adjusted pill probability is 23.7 percentage points above injectables (95% CI 13.9–32.4); injectables are 16.0 points above implants (13.1–18.7). The overall exploratory test of method differences has p<0.001. None of the five demographic characteristics shows clear overall variation at the 0.05 level in exploratory omnibus tests. For example, parity 0 is 9.1 points above parity 3+, but its direct 95% CI (-0.6–17.1) includes zero. These tests were not corrected for multiple comparisons, and lack of statistical evidence does not establish equality.

The IUD and Other modern rows have smaller samples; Other modern combines several distinct methods. Their estimates are less precise than those for injectables, implants, and pills.

Eligible episodes include those that continued, were censored, or ended for any reason. The weighted episode count is not a count of unique women. Unadjusted subgroup confidence intervals are not shown; the confidence intervals belong to the adjusted estimates. Differences between groups should be assessed using uncertainty in the difference, not by whether their individual intervals overlap.

Risk-model methods and interpretation

The Ethiopia DHS 2024–25 IR calendar contributes 8,611 eligible modern-method episodes excluding emergency contraception, including 7,648 observed starts, and 70,691 at-risk episode-months. The monthly outcome has three categories: continuing the original method, ending it for a selected reason, or ending it because of wanted pregnancy or partner disapproval. An episode leaves the risk set after either type of ending; otherwise it contributes observed months until censoring or month 12. Episodes already underway when calendar observation begins enter at their observed duration.

We fitted a survey-weighted multinomial discrete-time model with month since method start, original method, age and parity at start, education, wealth, residence, region, and year of start. Eligible late-entry episodes contribute only their observed months to the model fit, following the DHS life-table risk-set convention. For each subgroup, predicted monthly probabilities of both types of ending were combined into a 12-month cumulative incidence, then standardized over the same weighted distribution of episodes observed from method initiation. The woman's v005 weight is applied to each episode. Confidence intervals resample v021 PSUs within v022 strata, keeping all episodes from the same woman together.

Parity at method start was reconstructed from each woman's live-birth dates. Education, wealth, residence and region were measured at interview and may not reflect their values when an earlier episode began. These are adjusted associations, not causal effects. The overall standardized model estimate of 24.0% is close to the 23.6% selected-reason life-table estimate for the full EC-excluded modern-method cohort; they differ because the model uses complete cases and standardizes over observed starts.

Regional discontinuation and reason profiles

Regional denominators and precision

The left chart uses 8,611 eligible modern-method episodes excluding emergency contraception, age 15–49 at method start. The national comparison is 38.9%. Confidence intervals account for v005 weights, v021 PSUs and v022 strata. Region is residence at interview and may differ from residence when an earlier episode began. Each region’s reason percentages use its observed first-year discontinuations as denominator and sum to 100% before rounding. Method experience or access combines method failure, bleeding changes, other side effects or health concerns, wanted a more effective method, and access/cost/inconvenience.

All regions except Somali have 194–1,155 eligible episodes and 71–324 observed first-year discontinuations. That supports broad regional rate comparisons with confidence intervals; it does not make small differences or rare reason categories precise. Somali has 30 episodes and 11 first-year discontinuations. Partner disapproval has only 0–6 observed events per region, so its region-specific percentages are unstable. For the other three categories outside Somali, the median design-based 95% confidence interval is about 16 percentage points wide; use broad patterns rather than fine rankings.

mCPR and discontinuation: four regional profiles

What remains to classify regions

The typology is a decision framework, not an estimated regional classification. It requires regional modern contraceptive prevalence and a separately defined discontinuation-while-in-need measure. The all-cause original-method rate in the preceding figure cannot substitute for discontinuation while in need because it includes intended pregnancy and immediate method switching. Regional uncertainty should be checked before applying cut points.

After stopping for reasons other than wanting pregnancy, 67% had no method one month later

Both pathway figures use the same 2,620 observed modern-method discontinuation events (2,814.3 weighted). They exclude episodes stopped because the woman wanted to become pregnant, retain pregnancy while using, and require 12 subsequent calendar months of observation.

Modern use rises by month 12, but nonuse persists

A 60-month woman-level view reveals the pathways between snapshots

The month-12 endpoint does not distinguish continuous nonuse from a gap, restart, or later exit. The next two slides show how the five-year calendar can be used to classify women by their observed sequence, estimate each trajectory’s share of women, and compare the characteristics of women in each group. The sequences below are illustrative candidate patterns; trajectory groups and their sizes have not yet been estimated.

Candidate five-year contraceptive trajectories

From discontinuation to sustained mCPR growth

These closing slides connect method starts and current use to the number of women using a modern method at year end. The current-user illustration scales Ethiopia’s survey mCPR (22.7% among women 15–49) and the standardized historical pathway to one million women of reproductive age: about 64,000 baseline current users would experience a gross modern-use exit, and about 53,000 would remain outside modern use at month 12. These are a stationary-behavior scenario, not a national forecast. They exclude modern-use entrants and population change; full uncertainty and sensitivity analysis remain to be added. The method-by-reason population counts remain planned outputs.

Current users scaled to women of reproductive age

Separate official-mCPR scenario: The following current-user projection retains EC in the DHS current-use definition; it has not been re-estimated as an EC-excluded ongoing-method scenario. The scenario uses the survey-date method and duration mix of current users. The weighted mix is 57.6% injectables, 29.6% implants, 7.1% pill, and 5.8% other modern including EC. This differs from the all-start initiation mix, which includes modern-to-modern switches and restarts. It cannot be substituted for the method mix among genuinely new modern-use entrants.

Planned counts by method and stated reason

How to read this comparison

The gold bars show each method’s share of weighted contraceptive starts excluding EC. The navy bars show the weighted 12-month probability of ending that original method. The measures use different episode cohorts: observed starts for method mix and a life table that also includes eligible episodes already underway for discontinuation. Ending a method includes immediate switching, so the navy bars do not measure exit from all modern use. Other modern excludes EC and IUD.

Estimates, survey design and DHS validation

The primary initiation figures cover 7,900 observed contraceptive method starts (9,214.3 weighted) during months 3–62 before interview, excluding EC. Of those, 7,699 starts (8,993.7 weighted) are modern methods other than EC; 201 starts (220.6 weighted) are traditional methods. Starts include switches and restarts, but not use episodes already underway before the observation window. Each annual bar uses that year's EC-excluded weighted starts as its denominator; 2024 has partial coverage.

Why starts and life-table episodes differ: the presentation's EC-excluded discontinuation life table contains 8,682 eligible episodes (10,035.7 weighted). An episode can contribute to the life table even if it began before the observation window, provided it contributes observed exposure during the first 12 months of method use. These are neither 10,035.7 observed starts nor 10,035.7 observed discontinuations.

Weighting and scope: shares use the woman's DHS weight v005/1,000,000. Reading the table: The N is the unweighted episode count; its percentage is the survey-weighted share among these 417 eligible “Other” episodes. Each month cell gives a snapshot of states and sums to 100% before rounding. Zero-valued states are omitted. “Original method again” means use of the same method at that checkpoint after a recorded stop; “different modern method” excludes emergency contraception. The pregnancy state combines pregnant, birth, and pregnancy termination. Emergency contraception was excluded as an originating method but can appear later.

Follow-up and precision: The full first-year “Other recorded reasons” group contains 527 observed endings; 110 had fewer than 12 subsequent calendar months and are excluded from this fixed-cohort table. Percentages use DHS individual weights. The difficulty/menopause row has only 8 episodes, so its percentages are unstable. The historical calendar does not record when sex resumed or a partner returned; these patterns cannot establish why use resumed. An episode is a recorded period of method use, and a woman may contribute multiple episodes.

Exploring which groups have higher 12-month discontinuation for selected reasons

This exploratory analysis uses the same outcome as the selected-reason regression: among all eligible ongoing-modern-method episodes, excluding EC, the 12-month cumulative probability of ending the original method for any recorded reason other than wanting to become pregnant or husband/partner disapproval. Those two endings remain competing events in every group's life table. The overall unadjusted probability on this complete-case cohort is 23.6% across 8,611 eligible episodes. The estimand is an episode risk, not the proportion of discontinued episodes or a current-user next-year forecast.

One characteristic at a time

Age and parity refer to the start of each method episode. Parity uses the requested 0, 1–2, 3, and 4+ live-birth groups. Marital status, education, wealth and residence were measured at interview, sometimes years after the episode; their subgroup patterns must not be read as causal effects of those characteristics at method start. Method refers to the original method and includes subsequent switches as endings.

Reading high estimates: the unadjusted probability is 46.7% for pill episodes and 31.0% for parity-zero episodes. The never-in-union group is high but includes only 173 eligible episodes. These categories overlap; they cannot be added or interpreted as independent drivers. Confidence intervals and subgroup sample sizes should accompany any comparison.

Extended regression with marital status

The adjusted column in the one-way tables comes from a survey-weighted, three-outcome discrete-time model. Its monthly outcomes are continuing the original method, ending it for a retained reason, or ending it because of wanted pregnancy or partner disapproval. It controls simultaneously for method, duration month, age at start, parity at start (0, 1–2, 3, 4+), marital status at interview, education, wealth, residence, region, and method-start year. Eligible late-entry episodes contribute their observed duration months to the fit; predicted rates standardize each group to a common weighted distribution of episodes observed from initiation. The model converged, and its standardized overall selected-reason probability is 24.0%.

Inference boundary: the adjusted estimates are point estimates only. The earlier regression's confidence intervals and significance tests belong to its previous specification and cannot be reused after adding marital status and splitting parity. We have not yet calculated design-based intervals or direct contrasts for this extended model. Marital status at interview may have changed after a historical method episode, so even an adjusted association is not evidence that current marital status caused that earlier ending. Apparent differences across many one-way and two-way groups are exploratory and subject to multiple comparisons.

Full adjusted model with four parity and four marital-status categories

The adjusted columns in the one-way tables above belong to the earlier exploratory specification, which used 0, 1–2, 3, and 4+ for parity and three marital-status groups. The revised model results below use 0, 1–2, 3–4, and 5+ for parity and the four requested marital-status groups.

Outcome: the 12-month cumulative probability of discontinuing the original modern method for any recorded reason except wanted pregnancy or husband/partner disapproval. Those two excluded endings remain competing events in the monthly risk sets. Switching after ending the original method can still count as discontinuation here.

This model includes duration month, original method, age and parity at method start (0, 1–2, 3–4, 5+), marital status at interview (never in union, living together, married, divorced/widowed/separated), education, household wealth, residence, region, and method-start year. It uses 8,611 eligible episodes and 1,837 selected first-year endings. The overall adjusted 12-month probability is 24.0% (95% CI 21.7–26.1%).

Interpretation: These are standardized 12-month cumulative probabilities, not odds ratios. Age and parity refer to method start; marital status, education, wealth, residence, and region were measured at interview and could have changed since a historical episode. The never-in-union and living-together categories each have 173 eligible episodes, with wider intervals. An adjusted association is not evidence that marital status caused discontinuation.

Wealth-quintile sensitivity model

To assess whether the three-group wealth adjustment hides a gradient, the identical full model was refitted with all five DHS combined-wealth quintiles: poorest, poorer, middle, richer, and richest. Every other outcome definition and covariate stayed the same. The overall adjusted probability is 24.0% (95% CI 21.7–26.1%).

CovariateGroupEligible episodesUnadjusted 12-month probabilityAdjusted 12-month probabilityAdjusted 95% CI
DHS wealth quintilePoorest1,19423.8%27.3%21.2–34.0%
Poorer1,48419.7%23.3%19.2–27.2%
Middle1,46523.3%26.2%21.9–31.3%
Richer1,60223.8%24.7%20.8–28.1%
Richest2,86625.4%21.9%17.4–26.8%

The adjusted poorest-minus-richest difference is 5.4 percentage points (95% bootstrap CI -2.9 to 14.2 points). The pattern across all five categories should be assessed as a whole rather than inferred from this one contrast alone.

Wealth is the DHS asset-based index measured at interview, not income at method start. These category-specific intervals are useful for assessing precision; the poorest-versus-richest contrast does not by itself establish a monotonic gradient across all five quintiles.

Reasons by covariate subgroup

A narrower descriptive companion to the model above. The adjusted regression above counts endings for every recorded reason other than wanted pregnancy and partner disapproval. This table also excludes Other fertility-related and Other recorded reasons from the outcome. All four excluded ending categories remain competing events in the original risk set; no episodes are deleted because of how they ended. Method failure remains included. These descriptive probabilities are therefore lower than those in the regression above and must not be read as adjusted model results.

The five reason percentages on each row rank contributions to that row’s 12-month retained-reason probability. They sum to 100% after rounding. Each is a reason-specific cumulative incidence divided by the row’s retained-reason total, not a raw percentage of observed endings. The table has 95% survey-design intervals for the total probability; the individual reason shares have no intervals.

A simple example: age at method start
Age at startRetained-reason ending by 3 monthsBy 6 monthsBy 12 monthsBleeding or other side effects by 12 monthsStill using original method at 12 months

25-345.8%9.5%17.4%6.2%62.1%
35-497.4%11.5%18.5%6.8%64.3%

How to read ages 15–19: Among eligible episodes begun at ages 15–19, an estimated 6.5% ended for a retained reason by month 3 and 18.2% by month 12; 5.5% ended because of bleeding or other side effects by month 12. An estimated 55.1% were still using the original method at month 12. This is not the same as still using any contraception: switching ends the original method episode. The two rightmost columns are not complements. These time-point values are unadjusted point estimates; only the 12-month retained-reason total in the full table below has a displayed confidence interval.

Top three reasons by subgroup and original method

The all-modern, injectable, pill and IUD tables apply the same five-reason outcome as the descriptive table immediately above. Each reason cell reports both its absolute 12-month cumulative probability among all eligible episodes in that row and its share of that row’s retained-reason probability. Rankings use the absolute probabilities. They are unadjusted; they are not evidence that a covariate causes discontinuation.

The IUD has only 18 observed retained first-year endings overall. Rankings are withheld for subgroup cells with fewer than 10 retained endings, and † flags 10–29 endings as tentative. No reason-specific confidence intervals or formal subgroup contrasts are available for these rankings. The complete table is retained here for exploration, but a presentation should focus on better-supported method and subgroup comparisons.

Do distinct high-, medium-, and low-risk profiles emerge?

Only a lower-risk group is clearly distinguishable. A model trained outside each episode’s survey cluster placed episodes into three equally weighted bands of predicted 12-month original-method discontinuation for reasons other than wanted pregnancy or partner disapproval. The middle and higher bands overlap statistically. These are episode-level risk strata, not natural clusters of women or longitudinal trajectory groups.

Predicted-risk bandEligible episodesWeighted shareObserved 12-month probability95% survey-bootstrap interval
Lower3,18333.4%12.4%10.2–14.8%
Middle2,52133.3%27.5%23.3–31.5%
Higher2,90733.3%30.7%27.8–33.7%

Observed differences between bands, estimated directly rather than judged from overlapping intervals:

ComparisonDifference95% survey-bootstrap interval
Middle minus Lower15.0 percentage points11.0 to 19.1 points
Higher minus Middle3.2 percentage points-1.4 to 7.9 points
Higher minus Lower18.2 percentage points14.5 to 22.0 points

The lowest band differs clearly from both other bands. The higher-minus-middle interval includes zero, so these data do not support calling them separate medium- and high-risk clusters. The model uses original method, age, and parity at the start of the method episode. Method largely defines the separation: lower-risk episodes are predominantly implants, middle-risk episodes are almost entirely injectables, and the higher band includes pills and injectables.

Who is in each predicted-risk band?

Each percentage below is the share within that band, not the discontinuation probability for that characteristic. Method, age, and parity helped construct the score, so their concentration across bands is partly built into the classification.

CharacteristicCategoryLowerMiddleHigher
Original methodImplants75.9%0.0%0.0%
Original methodInjectables20.4%98.4%66.5%
Original methodPill0.0%0.0%28.0%
Age at method start15-199.5%5.4%22.5%
Age at method start25-3453.8%41.7%38.8%
Parity at method start06.2%1.8%28.0%
Parity at method start1-237.7%62.7%42.2%
Marital status at interviewNever in union2.1%0.8%4.2%

Method: 8,611 eligible modern-method episodes, excluding emergency contraception. Wanted pregnancy and partner disapproval remain competing endings. Five validation folds were assigned by survey cluster within survey stratum. Rates are weighted 12-month competing-risk life-table probabilities with delayed entry. The 95% intervals use 400 survey-stratified cluster bootstrap replicates with scores and risk cut-points held fixed; they therefore do not capture model-training uncertainty. Multiple episodes can belong to one woman. Marital status is measured at interview and may differ from status when an earlier episode began. These are descriptive/predictive associations, not causal explanations or evidence of naturally occurring latent classes.

12-month selected-reason discontinuation by method-duration class

Grouping methods makes the short-acting and long-acting comparison more stable while preserving the same competing-risk definition used above. These probabilities describe ending the original method for any recorded reason except wanted pregnancy or husband/partner disapproval. Endings for those two excluded reasons remain competing events in the risk set.

Method categoryMethods includedEligible episodesSelected endings12-month probability95% CI
Short-acting reversibleInjectables, pills, male condoms5,7081,53728.5%26.0–31.0%
Long-acting reversibleImplants, IUDs2,69025310.2%8.0–12.9%
Other modern, mixedLAM, sterilization, unspecified other modern2134725.5%11.6–41.2%

Can we still disaggregate by reason? Yes. For each category, estimate a separate 12-month cumulative incidence for each recorded reason using the same group of eligible episodes at baseline; all other endings remain competing events. The reason-specific probabilities then sum to the category's selected-reason total before rounding. They must not be calculated only among episodes that ended, because that would answer a different question: the distribution of reasons among discontinuations.

The short-acting group has 1,537 selected first-year endings, supporting a detailed reason breakdown. The implant/IUD group has 253, but some individual reasons are sparse (only one recorded method-failure ending); aggregate sparse reasons or show wide intervals. The remaining mixed category has 47 selected endings and should not be interpreted as a coherent method-duration class or broken into many reason-specific rates. EC is excluded. Estimates use survey weights and 400 survey-stratified PSU bootstrap replicates; repeated episodes from a woman share a survey cluster. Ending the original method can include switching, so these are not necessarily exits from modern contraception.

Why short-acting methods ended within 12 months

Among 1,537 recorded first-year endings of injectable, pill, or male-condom episodes for a reason other than wanted pregnancy or husband/partner disapproval, the survey-weighted distribution of reasons is below. Percentages sum to 100% before rounding. The denominator is these discontinuations, not all short-acting method episodes.

Recorded reasonEndings (N)Weighted share95% CI
Wanted a more effective method35023.3%19.0–28.0%
Other fertility-related reasons28420.6%16.0–25.3%
Access / cost / inconvenience30318.4%15.3–21.5%
Other side effects / health concerns18512.5%8.6–16.9%
Changes in menstrual bleeding17811.8%9.2–14.8%
Other recorded reasons1769.3%6.0–12.9%
Method failure / pregnancy while using614.1%2.4–6.1%

“Other fertility-related” includes infrequent sex or husband away, difficulty becoming pregnant or menopause, and marital dissolution or separation. “Other recorded” comprises residual other, unknown, or missing reasons. A reason's share among endings does not equal its 12-month probability among everyone using a short-acting method. Confidence intervals use 400 survey-stratified cluster bootstrap replicates; the weighted number of selected endings is 1,733.7.

Subnational discontinuation analysis_extension

This extension brings the regional estimates and diagnostics together: where original modern methods end, how much regional rates change after adjustment, which recorded reasons account for endings, and whether method mix or within-method risk helps explain regional gaps. The selected-reason analyses treat wanted pregnancy and husband/partner disapproval as competing endings; the all-cause comparisons include every recorded reason. These two outcomes must be read separately.

Regional 12-month discontinuation for reasons other than wanted pregnancy or partner disapproval

These figures use the same 8,611 complete-case, emergency-contraception-excluded modern-method episodes. On the left, they show the 12-month probability that the original method ends for a recorded reason other than wanted pregnancy or husband/partner disapproval. Those excluded endings remain in the initial eligible population and are treated as competing events. The aligned panels on the right show reason distributions among observed first-year endings. Regions have the same order in both figures and in the tables below, ranked by the unadjusted probability except that Somali is placed last because its sample is small.

Unadjusted 12-month discontinuation by region

Which unadjusted regional rates differ?

Formal pairwise tests compare the 12-month unadjusted competing-risk probabilities in all 91 pairs of 14 regions. Each test uses 10,000 stratified survey-PSU bootstrap replicates, with the same PSU draw applied to both regions and all episodes from a woman kept together. The two-sided p-value uses the null-centered bootstrap distribution of the direct rate difference. Holm adjustment controls the familywise error rate across all 91 tests. 3 pairs remain significant at 0.05 after Holm adjustment: Tigray versus Amhara, Sidama, and South West Ethiopia. A positive difference means the first region has the higher rate. Pointwise 95% intervals and regional sample sizes are included in the downloadable results; those intervals are not adjusted for multiple comparisons.

How to use “Other”: Its national share is 28.1% of retained first-year discontinuations, so it matters for interpreting the profile and for checking reason coding. It is not a single preventable-service category: 53.0% of this Other group is coded as infrequent sex or husband away, while marital dissolution, difficulty becoming pregnant, fatalistic answers, unspecified responses, and unknown reasons make up the rest. The original-code breakdown and follow-up states help distinguish a temporary pause from prolonged nonuse. Any service response should support informed contraceptive choice rather than assume that every stop needs preventing, consistent with WHO's guidance on contraceptive choice.

Regional method starts and 12-month discontinuation

These five exploratory figures repeat the presentation's paired-bar format for each method, with the national benchmark above the regions. The gold bar is the method's share of all observed contraceptive starts in that region, excluding emergency contraception from both numerator and denominator but retaining traditional-method starts in the denominator. The navy bar is the survey-weighted 12-month probability of ending that original method for any recorded reason, including switching. Its life-table denominator is eligible episodes of the named method, including delayed-entry episodes; it is not the start-share denominator.

Regional navy bars are withheld where there are fewer than 30 eligible method episodes or fewer than 10 observed first-year endings. All regional IUD rates fall below this display threshold; other-modern rates are available in only a few regions. These descriptive bars have no confidence intervals and should not be used to claim significant regional differences. The selected-reason regional analysis elsewhere on this page uses a different outcome.

View the underlying values for the five regional figures

Gold-bar shares use all observed contraceptive starts, including traditional methods, excluding EC. Navy bars use eligible episodes of that method to estimate all-cause 12-month discontinuation. The two denominators differ.

Does method mix explain the Tigray–Amhara gap?

In the descriptive comparison, Tigray has a smaller injectable share among eligible episodes than Amhara, yet higher discontinuation among injectable episodes. Reweighting both regions to a common method mix increases the gap. This separates a method-mix component from within-method differences; it does not establish why those differences arise.

RegionMethodEligible episodesSelected endingsShare of eligible episodes (%)Selected-reason 12-month probability (%)

TigrayPill53207.942.2
TigrayOther modern2473.442.3
AmharaInjectables70812970.321.5
AmharaImplants1811218.07.2
AmharaPill954110.155.2
AmharaOther modern1511.66.3
Gap componentContribution (percentage points)95% CI (percentage points)
Method mix-3.2-5.0 to -1.5
Within-method risk12.67.7 to 17.5

The method-mix analysis combines IUD and other modern methods in a small residual group. Its common-mix estimates approximate, rather than exactly reproduce, the original regional life-table probabilities. See the visual regional diagnostic for the complete explanation.

What is being decomposed?

The outcome here is the 12-month probability of ending the original modern-method episode for any recorded reason other than wanted pregnancy or husband/partner disapproval. Those two excluded endings remain competing events in the life table. We compare two distinct quantities for each method: its survey-weighted share of eligible episodes in the region and its 12-month selected-reason discontinuation probability among episodes using that method. The shares are not shares of observed new starts or of women currently using contraception. The method-specific probabilities are calculated from monthly weighted risk sets, not by dividing the count of endings by the count of episodes.

Worked example: Tigray and Amhara

Injectables account for 56.7% of Tigray’s eligible method episodes and 70.3% of Amhara’s. Yet the 12-month selected-reason discontinuation probability among injectable episodes is 38.8% in Tigray and 21.5% in Amhara. The underlying unweighted injectable samples are 388 and 708 episodes, respectively. The shares use DHS survey weights; they are not 388 divided by 682 or 708 divided by 999.

First, using each region’s own method mix, multiply each method’s weighted episode share by its within-method probability and add across methods. For injectables, the contribution is approximately 0.567 × 38.8% = 22.0 percentage points in Tigray and 0.703 × 21.5% = 15.1 points in Amhara. Adding implants, pills, and other modern methods gives a weighted-average probability of 31.4% for Tigray and 22.1% for Amhara, a 9.3-point gap.

Next, give the two regions the same mix: the simple average of their weighted method shares, method by method. The common injectable share is (56.7% + 70.3%) ÷ 2 = 63.5%. This is an equally weighted comparison standard, not the pooled share of all episodes in the two regions. Keep each region’s own within-method probabilities. Under that common mix, the weighted-average probabilities are 33.1% for Tigray and 20.6% for Amhara, a 12.6-point gap.

Method-mix comparisonTigrayAmharaTigray minus Amhara
Each region’s own mix31.4%22.1%9.3 percentage points
Same average method mix33.1%20.6%12.6 percentage points

What is the statistical technique, and why average?

This is a symmetric two-factor Kitagawa decomposition of a difference between weighted averages, not a fitted Oaxaca–Blinder regression. It assigns −3.2 percentage points to differences in method mix and +12.6 points to differences in probabilities within methods; together these equal the 9.3-point own-mix gap. The symmetric calculation averages the two regions’ method shares when assessing within-method rates and averages their method-specific rates when assessing mix. Neither region is chosen as the reference, and the components add exactly for this weighted-average comparison. Using Amhara’s mix alone as the reference would be a legitimate alternative question but would allocate the overlap between composition and rates differently. See the , , and . For the general method, see the U.S. Census Bureau standardization and decomposition manual.

The negative mix component means Tigray’s method mix reduces its gap with Amhara. In particular, Tigray has a lower injectable share even though injectable discontinuation is much higher there. At the common injectable share of 63.5%, the 17.4-point difference between the regions’ injectable probabilities contributes approximately 0.635 × 17.4 = 11.0 points to the within-method gap. The other methods bring that component to 12.6 points overall. This identifies higher within-method discontinuation, especially for injectables, as a lead for investigation; it does not establish a service-delivery cause.

Why these numbers differ slightly from the regional life-table graph: the direct all-method competing-risk estimates are 31.7% for Tigray and 22.1% for Amhara, about a 9.6-point gap. The decomposition exactly partitions the 9.3-point gap between weighted averages of separate method-specific life tables. That weighted average need not reproduce an all-method life table exactly because the monthly risk sets and censoring differ. The small Other-modern cells are also unstable: this decomposition pools IUD with that group and should remain exploratory.

What analysis would better test and explain regional differences?

Use the direct survey-weighted regional cumulative incidences, confidence intervals, and planned pairwise contrasts to establish which gaps are distinguishable from sampling variation. Then report marginally standardized 12-month probabilities from a competing-event model so regions are compared on the same distribution of method, duration, age, parity, education, wealth, residence, and start year. The already does this with main effects, but it does not include region-by-method interactions or marital status, and its parity grouping is 0, 1–2, and 3+. A next model could add region-by-method interactions to test whether, for example, injectable discontinuation differs between regions after adjustment, while pooling sparse cells where necessary. Timing, stated reasons, switching after stopping, and credible service or supply measures could then be examined as possible explanations. An adjusted association or persistent residual gap does not by itself identify a causal driver; region is recorded at interview and may not be the episode’s location at initiation.

Each cell is the cumulative selected-reason probability by that month, with a 95% survey-bootstrap interval. Wanted pregnancy and partner disapproval remain competing endings. Somali has too few eligible injectable episodes for a useful regional estimate.

Expected-user discontinuation calculations

These final tables translate method-ending probabilities into illustrative counts among current users. The first set applies first-year risks observed after method starts; the second accounts for how long current users have already used their methods. Both describe endings of the original method, which can include switching rather than an exit from modern contraception.

Illustrative current-user method endings for reasons other than wanted pregnancy or partner disapproval

These tables estimate how many current users might end their original method for a recorded reason other than wanting to become pregnant or husband/partner disapproval. The probability in each row is the cause-specific 12-month cumulative incidence from the same competing-risk life table as the selected-reason analysis above. The seven retained probabilities sum to the selected-reason probability for that method, for example 25.54% for injectables. The tables are planning illustrations, not prospective forecasts.

Inputs used throughout: Ethiopia 2025 women aged 15–49 (WRA): 34,057,556 from the frozen UN/WDI planning denominator; survey-weighted 2024–25 Ethiopia DHS all-women mCPR: 22.7% (unrounded 22.7287%); estimated current modern users: 7,740,836. Current method distribution comes from the survey-date DHS IR current-method variable v312, weighted by v005; it is not the calendar initiation mix. Each method's 12-month probabilities come from the weighted, competing-risk contraceptive-calendar life table used for the Table 7.11 replication. For each reason, the denominator is all eligible episodes starting that method; other endings are competing events. Emergency contraception represents 0.85% of current modern users but has no ongoing-use discontinuation projection here.

Outcome definition: The two excluded reason types, wanted pregnancy and husband/partner disapproval, remain in the starting risk set and leave it when they occur; they are competing events, not episodes deleted from the denominator. The seven retained reasons include method failure/pregnancy while using, other fertility-related reasons, residual Other recorded reasons, bleeding, other side effects/health concerns, wanting a more effective method, and access/cost/inconvenience. An ending followed by switching still counts as ending the original method; these numbers do not measure exits from all modern contraception.

Estimating next-year method endings among current users

Which probability answers which question?

ApproachProbability usedWhat it tells usAppropriate use
First year after method start: tables immediately aboveReason-specific cumulative probability during months 1–12 after a method starts, per all eligible starting episodes.How new method episodes end in their first year. The current-user counts above apply that new-start risk to every user of the same method.Compare methods and reasons for new starts; treat the current-user counts as an illustration, not a next-year forecast.
Next 12 months after time already used: tables belowReason-specific probability in the next 12 observed months among historical users who had already continued 0–12 or 13+ months on that method. Weight the two probabilities by today's users in those duration groups.How current users may end their original method over the coming year, conditional on their method and time already using it.More relevant for planning support for the existing user stock; still preliminary because historical patterns may change and small cells limit precision.

How the current tables were constructed: the tables immediately above use the Ethiopia DHS calendar's method-and-reason-specific 12-month cumulative incidences from method initiation. They keep all eligible episodes in the original risk set, treat wanted pregnancy and husband/partner disapproval as competing events, and add the seven retained reason probabilities. Multiplying that sum by the estimated number of current users of each method assumes that every current user faces the risk observed just after initiation. That assumption does not account for duration already used.

How the suggested tables were constructed: the historical landmark cohort contains women using a modern method 15 months before interview, leaving 12 observed follow-up months. We measure their time already using the original method from the monthly calendar, record the first original-method ending and its stated reason over the next 12 months, and estimate weighted probabilities separately for 0–12 and 13+ months already used. Wanted pregnancy and partner disapproval remain in each baseline denominator as competing endings. We apply these two reason-specific probabilities to the corresponding weighted shares of survey-date users of each method (current method v312, duration v337, weight v005). Counts use the same 2025 WRA and all-women mCPR inputs documented above; EC is excluded. A method's seven reason probabilities sum to its selected-reason next-12-month probability.

Provisional estimates: these are historical, duration-standardized method-ending estimates, not observed future outcomes or net exits from modern contraception. A direct switch remains an original-method ending. We use only two duration groups because some detailed groups are sparse. Historical N in the 0–12 and 13+ groups are Injectables: 658 and 1102; Implants: 416 and 853; Pill: 85 and 100; IUD: 22 and 72; Other modern methods: 58 and 53. The smallest cell is IUD users at 0–12 months (N=22), and several reason-specific event counts are under 10 (marked †). No confidence intervals are available yet; these estimates should not be treated as precise forecasts or used for fine program targeting without a pooled model and uncertainty analysis.

Interpretation: these probabilities estimate ending the original method in the next 12 months for a retained reason.
