Updated subnational heterogeneity analyses

How the subnational probabilities handle late entries: The weighted competing-risk life tables retain eligible episodes already underway at the beginning of the 3–62-month observation window and add them only at their observed method-use duration. The current adjusted-model preview also uses those episodes in the monthly regression risk sets. Its predicted rates are averaged over observed starts as a common baseline reference; this does not remove late entrants from model fitting, and subtracting a model-standardized rate from a raw life-table rate is not an exact method-mix decomposition. Review the model preview.

Before comparing regions, keep the recorded reason separate from an interpretive program grouping. The contraceptive calendar records one stated reason when an episode ends. The 15 responses below reproduce the categories used for the detailed Ethiopia DHS Table 7.12 analysis. The three program groupings are our analytic shorthand, not DHS classifications or proof that any individual discontinuation was preventable.

Interpretive grouping Recorded reasons in the contraceptive calendar How to use the grouping
Stated pregnancy intention
Previously described as “not a program failure”		 Wanted to become pregnant. A planned decision to stop should not be counted as a preventable service failure. The calendar reason alone does not establish every aspect of intent or future contraceptive need.
Potentially actionable Became pregnant while using the method (method failure); wanted a more effective method; changes in menstrual bleeding; other side effects or health concerns; lack of access or too far; cost too much; inconvenient to use; and husband/partner disapproved. These responses can motivate investigation of counseling, method choice, side-effect support, access, resupply, and respectful response to partner concerns. “Potentially actionable” is not an estimated preventable fraction. Wanting a more effective method may be followed by a successful switch. Partner disapproval calls for particular care around autonomy and safety.
Circumstantial or unresolved Up to God/fatalistic; difficult to get pregnant or menopausal; infrequent sex or husband away; marital dissolution or separation; Other (unspecified); and Don't know. These reasons do not identify a single service failure. Infrequent sex or partner absence need not mean contraceptive need has ended permanently. “Other” and “Don't know” are unresolved responses, not confirmed circumstances; examine them separately where sample size permits.

Important distinction between analyses: The presentation’s broad “potentially actionable” grouping included husband/partner disapproval. The current primary selected-reason 12-month discontinuation analysis excludes both wanted pregnancy and partner disapproval as outcomes; they remain competing events in its life table. Its selected total therefore still includes method failure, the three other fertility-related responses, and the residual Other/Don't know responses. A narrower method experience or access outcome retains only method failure, wanting a more effective method, bleeding changes, other side effects/health concerns, lack of access, cost, and inconvenience. Do not label either selected total as the number of preventable discontinuations or women lost from modern contraception.

Next subnational display: For each region, show the 12-month cause-specific probability for each sufficiently supported reason among all eligible modern-method episodes, then a separate heatmap of reason shares among selected discontinuations. State the denominator on both. A focused injectable breakdown and follow-up switching/non-use analysis can follow where regional event counts and confidence intervals support interpretation.

Regional discontinuation for method experience or access

This repeats the regional 12-month analysis using a narrower outcome: ending a modern-method episode because of method failure, wanting a more effective method, bleeding changes, other side effects or health concerns, or access/cost/convenience. The last category combines lack of access or too far, cost too much, and inconvenient to use. Wanted pregnancy, husband/partner disapproval, other fertility-related reasons, and Other/Don't know do not count toward this outcome; they remain competing endings. Method failure includes becoming pregnant while using. The denominator is all eligible modern-method episodes, including episodes that later end for excluded reasons. Estimates describe ending the original method, not necessarily leaving modern contraception.

The left panel shows the survey-weighted 12-month cumulative probability with 95% bootstrap intervals. The right panel shows the contribution of each retained reason to that probability, divided by the regional total. Each heatmap row therefore adds to 100%; it is not a raw count distribution of observed endings. Nationally, the selected-reason probability is 17.0% across 8,611 eligible episodes.

Reading the two panels: A high share in a heatmap cell does not itself mean a high cause-specific risk. Compare it with the left-hand regional total; the cause-specific 12-month probability for every region and reason is available in the data download. These estimates identify where to investigate method experience and access, not which interruptions were preventable or how many women exited all modern contraception.

Which actionable reasons dominate within each method and region?

These five heatmaps split the existing regional analysis by the original modern method. Within each method and region, the five cells show the share of its 12-month probability of ending for a potentially actionable reason attributable to method failure, wanting a more effective method, bleeding changes, other side effects/health, or access/cost/convenience. Access/cost/convenience combines lack of access or too far, cost too much, and inconvenient to use. Other side effects/health includes other side effects or health concerns; menstrual bleeding changes are shown separately. The national benchmark row is gold. Shown rows add to 100.0%. This is a reason mix among actionable original-method endings, not the probability that all users discontinue for each reason. Other endings stay as competing events in the weighted life table.

View these heatmaps as slides 31–35 in the presentation preview · Open/download the updated PowerPoint.

Why the N values are smaller than Table 7.11: the main EC-excluded life table contains 8,682 unweighted eligible modern-method episodes (10,035.7 after weighting). These figures use 8,611 episodes with complete covariate data, then split them by original method and region. The actionable endings N counts only episodes observed ending within their first 12 months for one of the five retained reason groups; 1,321 such endings were observed nationally. It is an event count, not the number of episodes used as the life-table denominator. The 17.0% national risk comes from monthly weighted competing-risk calculations, so it is not 1,321 divided by 8,611.

An asterisk beside a region marks fewer than 30 eligible method episodes or fewer than 30 observed actionable first-year endings. Gray rows have fewer than 30 eligible episodes or fewer than 10 actionable endings, so their percentages are withheld. Exact counts remain in the underlying data. IUD has only 18 actionable endings nationally, so no regional IUD row supports a reason comparison. Other modern pools several methods and has only one displayable regional row. The injectable panel is the most useful regional diagnostic; implant and pill patterns merit caution where counts are small.

When and where method experience or access discontinuation occurs

The heatmap shows all 14 survey regions and the national benchmark. Regions are ordered by their observed 12-month estimate, except Somali, which is displayed last and not ranked because only five selected endings were observed. The neutral locator map shows where every region is. Differences in point estimates do not by themselves establish statistically significant regional differences; confidence intervals are in the regional table above and the underlying data below.

Each of the first four columns is the survey-weighted cumulative probability that an eligible modern-method episode has ended for method experience or access by 3, 6, 9, or 12 months. Wanted pregnancy, partner disapproval, and other excluded reasons remain competing endings. The final column is the 3-month probability divided by the 12-month probability: the share of first-year selected-reason risk concentrated in the first three months. It is not an additional discontinuation probability.

Which methods are initiated in each region?

Each stacked row shows the survey-weighted distribution of observed contraceptive method starts within that region; the national row is the benchmark. The seven categories and their colors match the original initiation-mix slide. Each row sums to 100% before rounding. Starts include switches and restarts, but exclude method episodes already underway when calendar observation begins. The chart does not show current use or the probability of discontinuation.

Method starts and potentially actionable discontinuation by region

The three paired-bar figures extend the national method-starts-versus-discontinuation slide to regions. Injectables, implants, and pills account for 92.0% of all observed contraceptive method starts nationally. Amber shows each method's share of all observed starts within the region, including emergency contraception and traditional methods in the denominator to match the initiation-mix figure immediately above. Navy shows the survey-weighted 12-month competing-risk probability that an eligible episode of that original modern method ends for method failure, wanting a more effective method, bleeding changes, other side effects or health concerns, or access/cost/convenience. Wanted pregnancy, partner disapproval, other fertility-related reasons, and other recorded/unknown reasons remain competing endings. The two bars have different denominators and should not be subtracted.

Nationally, the injectable method-ending probability is 44.5% for all reasons and 18.8% for potentially actionable reasons in this regional complete-case life-table cohort. The narrower estimate is a cause-specific probability; it does not imply that every such ending is preventable or that every ending represents a woman leaving modern contraception. The national start share of injectables is 58.8%.

Navy bars are withheld when fewer than 30 eligible method episodes or 10 potentially actionable first-year endings were observed in that region. IUD and other-modern regional rate panels were not added because none of the IUD regions and only one other-modern region meets this display rule. All counts and descriptive estimates remain in the underlying analysis table. Region is residence at interview and may differ from residence at method start; one woman can contribute multiple episodes.

Values in parentheses are 95% survey-stratified PSU bootstrap intervals: 1,500 replicates for the unadjusted delayed-entry competing-risk life table and 500 refits for adjusted models. All models fit the same 8,611 eligible episodes, but the adjusted probabilities are averaged over a common national set of 7,648 episodes observed from method start. The unadjusted rate includes all 8,611 episodes, including late entries, so raw-to-adjusted differences are not a pure measure of the effect of covariate adjustment. Model 1 accounts for method and month of use; Model 2 additionally accounts for age, parity, marital status, education, wealth, residence, and start year. Neither model includes a method-by-region interaction. Differences in point estimates do not by themselves establish statistical significance or causation.

Which regional differences remain after adjustment?

The global test asks whether the 12-month potentially actionable discontinuation probabilities are the same in every region. For the primary Model 2 comparison, its p-value is <0.001 across all 14 regions; omitting Somali, whose selected-ending sample is sparse, gives <0.001. A global result does not identify which pairs differ, so we also test all 91 region pairs directly.

What the models estimate

The analysis follows eligible modern-method use episodes month by month through month 12. Each observed episode-month has three possible outcomes: the original method continues; it ends for a potentially actionable reason (method failure, wanting a more effective method, bleeding or other side effects/health, or access/cost/convenience); or it ends for another reason. The last category includes wanted pregnancy, partner disapproval and other non-actionable endings. Those endings remain in the starting cohort and leave the risk set when they occur; they are competing events, not excluded episodes. An episode still using its original method at the end of observation is censored. Thus the reported rate is the cumulative probability of an actionable ending by 12 months among eligible modern-method episodes, not the share of discontinuers who gave an actionable reason.

Observed: a survey-weighted delayed-entry competing-risk life table estimates each region’s probability with its actual method and population composition. Model 1: a survey-weighted discrete-time multinomial regression models those three monthly outcomes using region, original method and month since method initiation. Month measures duration of the episode, not how long a current user had used a method at interview. Model 2: the same regression adds age and parity at episode start, marital status, education, wealth, urban/rural residence and calendar year of start. Region remains a predictor in both models. For each model, predicted monthly probabilities are combined into the 12-month competing-risk probability; to compare regions after adjustment, each region is evaluated over the same weighted set of 7,648 episodes observed from method start. This common comparison set changes the covariate profiles used for prediction, not the fitted region coefficient or the episodes used to fit the model. The fitted models use 8,611 eligible modern-method episodes, including 963 first observed after initiation.

Each difference subtracts two region-specific Model 2 probabilities averaged over the same measured observed-start profiles. The 500 survey-stratified PSU bootstrap refits resample entire clusters within strata and keep all episodes contributed by a woman together. The bootstrap standard error gives a two-sided Wald p-value; Holm correction controls the familywise error rate across all 91 pairs. The plotted interval uses the same bootstrap standard error with a Bonferroni critical value. This tests adjusted associations, not whether region itself caused the difference. A pair not highlighted here has insufficient evidence of a difference under this test; it is not proven equal.

After Holm correction, 2 of 91 observed pairs, 2 Model 1 pairs, and 2 fully adjusted Model 2 pairs pass the 5% threshold. The observed-rate tests use the delayed-entry, competing-risk life table and 10,000 survey-stratified PSU bootstrap draws. Model 1 and Model 2 each use 500 survey-stratified PSU model refits. These stages answer different questions because raw rates retain each region's actual composition, Model 1 accounts for method and duration, and Model 2 also standardizes measured population characteristics. The global tests and every pairwise result remain available for review.

At a 10% significance threshold: the primary fully adjusted Model 2 still identifies only Tigray versus Amhara (Holm-adjusted p=0.001) and Tigray versus Addis Ababa (p=0.005); no additional pair qualifies. Model 1 also identifies Central Ethiopia versus Addis Ababa (+9.9 percentage points; Holm-adjusted p=0.091), but this comparison does not qualify after full adjustment. Significance at 10% is weaker evidence than at 5%, and a non-significant comparison does not establish equal rates.

Do regional gaps remain when each pair has the same method mix?

Pair-specific comparison. For every pair of regions, we average those two regions’ survey-weighted shares of injectable, implant, pill, IUD, and other-modern starts. We then apply that same five-method mix to each region’s own modeled 12-month probability within each method. Thus neither region is assigned the national method mix. There is no single shared-mix rate per region: a region’s standardized rate changes with the region it is compared with.

The outcome is discontinuation of the original modern method within 12 months for a potentially actionable reason. Other endings, including wanted pregnancy and partner disapproval, remain competing events. Model 1 uses region, original method, and month since method start. It was fitted to 8,611 eligible EC-excluded modern-method episodes; the pairwise method shares come from 7,648 episodes observed from method start. This model-based comparison is separate from the raw DHS-style life-table rates shown above.

Reading the gap: “own mixes” shows the fitted difference with each region’s observed method distribution. “Shared mix” holds the distribution of the five methods equal within that pair while retaining each region’s fitted risk for each method. The method-mix contribution is the own-mix gap minus the shared-mix gap. These two components sum to the fitted own-mix gap by construction; they do not decompose a raw observed life-table difference.

All 91 regional pairs were tested using 500 survey-stratified PSU bootstrap refits and two-sided Wald tests. Holm adjustment controls the familywise error rate across the 91 comparisons. The table shows pairs meeting the 5% threshold. At the 10% familywise threshold, Central Ethiopia versus Addis Ababa (Holm p=0.077) also qualifies.

Interpretation and precision: A remaining same-mix gap means the five-method composition in Model 1 does not explain that modeled difference. It does not establish that services caused the difference. This additive model assumes a common regional logit shift across methods; it cannot show that one particular method drives the residual gap. Sparse regional method cells, especially Somali, IUD and other modern, make those comparisons imprecise. The next focused check is method-by-region risk for injectables and other methods with adequate data.

How do the clearest regional gaps change across three comparisons?

This figure follows three regional pairs that passed the 5% Holm-corrected threshold in at least one comparison. Each panel reports the higher region’s 12-month probability minus the lower region’s, in percentage points, for discontinuation of the original modern method for a potentially actionable reason. Pregnancy desire, partner disapproval, and other nonselected endings remain competing events.

Main finding: Tigray’s gap with Amhara and Addis Ababa remains statistically distinguishable in all three views; it does not disappear when method or measured population composition is aligned. Central Ethiopia’s gap with Amhara passes the corrected test only in the pair-shared-method-mix view. Lack of significance in the other views does not establish equal rates.

How the panels differ: “Observed” is the DHS-style delayed-entry competing-risk life-table gap. “Same method mix” uses Model 1’s region-specific, method-specific fitted risks and gives both regions in each pair the average of that pair’s two weighted method-start distributions. “Same methods + characteristics” uses the fully adjusted Model 2 and predicts each region for one common observed-start population with the same methods, age and parity at start, marital status, education, wealth, residence, and start period. The last panel uses a common population across all regions rather than a separate population for each pair. Thus the three gaps are informative comparisons, not successive pieces of an exact decomposition of the observed gap.

Does the regional gap depend on the method started?

This focused analysis compares Tigray, Amhara, Central Ethiopia, and Addis Ababa for injectables, implants, and pills. They were selected from earlier regional contrasts and sample-size checks. The outcome is ending the original method during the first year for a potentially actionable reason: method failure, wanting a more effective method, bleeding or other side effects/health concerns, and access/cost/convenience. Other endings stay as competing events. The survey-weighted monthly multinomial model adjusts for duration, age and parity at method start, marital status, education, wealth, residence, and method-start year. It was fitted to the 2,726 eligible episodes in these four regions and three methods. All displayed probabilities use the same weighted profile of 2,462 episodes observed from method start.

Formal test: The joint test of six 12-month method-by-region gap contrasts gives p = 0.471. For each prior regional pair, we compared the 12-month regional gap for injectables with the gap for implants and again with the gap for pills. Differences and uncertainty come from the same survey-stratified PSU bootstrap refits. Holm-adjusted p-values cover these six focused contrasts; this is an exploratory follow-up to pairs identified in the earlier analysis. None of the six focused tests establishes that a regional gap is different for injectables than for implants or pills after Holm correction. The estimated differences may still be meaningful; several method cells have limited events and wide intervals.

A difference between gaps is a statistical interaction on the 12-month probability scale. It does not identify why a method is discontinued. These adjusted estimates come from a focused four-region, three-method model, so they need not equal the earlier all-region Model 2 estimates. Small cells, especially pills in Tigray and Central Ethiopia and implant endings in Amhara and Addis Ababa, limit precision.

Do 12-month actionable discontinuation rates differ by region for injectables and implants?

These are observed, unadjusted survey-weighted 12-month cumulative probabilities of ending the original method for a potentially actionable reason. Each method has its own eligible episodes as the denominator: 4,796 injectable episodes (824 observed actionable first-year endings) and 2,522 implant episodes (191 endings). Wanted pregnancy, partner disapproval, and all other nonselected endings remain competing events. The monthly life table accommodates right censoring and episodes first observed after method start; its point estimates reproduce the existing regional method-rate table.

Global regional tests. We included regions with at least 100 eligible episodes and 10 observed actionable first-year endings for the method. For injectables, 12 regions met that criterion; the survey-bootstrap Wald test gives p = 0.007 and p = 0.013 after Holm correction across the two methods. For implants, 11 regions met it; p = 0.387. Thus the data support regional variation in the injectable probability; they do not establish regional variation for implants. An inconclusive implant test is not evidence that its true regional risks are identical.

Which regions differ? We tested every pair among the eligible regions for each method (66 injectable and 55 implant comparisons), using the same 5,000 survey-stratified PSU bootstrap samples and Holm correction across all 121 pairs. Only Tigray versus Amhara remained significant: 28.6% versus 15.3% for injectables, a difference of 13.4 percentage points (pointwise 95% bootstrap interval 7.0 to 19.6; corrected p = 0.003). No implant pair survived correction. The interval is pointwise; the corrected p-value is the multiple-comparison decision.

These tests describe unadjusted regional differences within each method. They do not control for differences in age, parity, or other characteristics of women using the method in each region, and they do not establish a causal regional effect. The implant comparisons have limited power because many regions have only 10–29 actionable endings.

Injectable regional gaps: observed, common method mix, and fully adjusted

This repeats the three-stage regional comparison specifically for injectable episodes and the 12-month probability of ending the original method for a potentially actionable reason. Wanted pregnancy, partner disapproval, and other nonselected endings remain competing events. The observed analysis uses the same survey-weighted DHS-style monthly life table as the method-specific rate figure above.

StageWhat is comparedTested regionsGlobal pPurpose
1. ObservedActual injectable episodes in each region120.007Is there an observed regional difference?
2. Same method mixIdentical to Stage 1: each episode is already injectable use120.007Method mix cannot explain differences within a single method.
3. Fully adjustedInjectable-only monthly competing-event model; compare regions using the same measured injectable-user profiles120.013How much regional variation remains after accounting for measured user composition?

Stage 3 includes month of use, region, age and parity at method start, marital status, education, wealth, residence, and method-start year. It fits 4,706 eligible injectable episodes in the 12 testable regions, including episodes observed after use began. To obtain comparable adjusted probabilities, it predicts each region’s 12-month risk for the same survey-weighted set of 4,156 injectable episodes observed from method start, then averages those predictions. This is adjustment for measured composition, not a causal estimate of a region’s health system.

Global tests: Stage 1 (and identical Stage 2) p = 0.007; Stage 3 p = 0.013. The fully adjusted global test also supports regional variation; measured population characteristics do not account for all observed variation. These global tests ask whether all 12 tested regional probabilities can be equal; they do not identify which pairs differ.

Regional pairs surviving multiple-comparison correction

For each distinct stage we tested all 66 region pairs using survey-stratified PSU bootstrap differences and Holm correction across those 66 comparisons. Stage 2 repeats Stage 1 exactly. The corrected p-values here use an injectable-only comparison family; they can differ from the previous table’s correction across injectable and implant pairs together.

Tigray vs Amhara13.4 pp (7.0 to 19.6)0.00213.7 pp (7.0 to 19.9)0.002

At 5%, the fully adjusted injectable-only analysis distinguishes only Tigray versus Amhara after correction across 66 pairs. Tigray versus South West Ethiopia and Tigray versus Sidama qualify at 10% (Holm p approximately 0.064 and 0.065) and remain exploratory. The adjusted intervals and tests use 500 valid survey-stratified PSU bootstrap refits. Adjustment does not by itself establish what caused a gap.

Ethiopia method discontinuation project · Consolidated analysis file

Three planning approaches: every actionable ending treated as a lost user

These main scenarios assume every potentially actionable original-method discontinuation is a lost modern user. Current users are estimated from the 2024–25 all-women survey prevalence and method mix, applied to 34,057,556 women aged 15–49 in 2025. Each 2026 scenario uses 35,016,171 women aged 15–49. mCPR increase in pp = users retained ÷ 35,016,171 × 100. EC remains in baseline mCPR but has no ongoing-method discontinuation probability. Switching and restarting are considered in the appendix.

Appendix: Sensitivity analysis if some users who discontinue switch

These tables deduct women using a modern method at year end after an actionable ending, including direct switchers and later restarters. The AFT model itself does not estimate switching.

  • Small samples: 126 of 200 method × cause × duration pathway cells require pooling; 48 pool across methods by cause. Fewer than 10 distinct episodes triggers pooling. Repeated calendar landmarks are not independent episodes.
  • Rare training events include IUD failure (1), other-modern bleeding (2) and implant failure (5). Individual cause estimates and pooled switching fractions require caution. Zero-event cells have no reliable bootstrap interval and are flagged †.
  • Historical 61+ months uses 37–60-month experience. AFT extrapolates selected survival curves; its predictor is method, while elapsed use sets the survival clock. Both assume historical patterns are informative about future users.
  • Year-end modern use includes EC, consistently with the mCPR definition. The original-method discontinuation cohort excludes EC. The sensitivity adjustment differs slightly from earlier tables because it now includes EC at the endpoint and assesses support using distinct episodes.
  • Intervals condition on fixed population and growth inputs and selected AFT distributions. They do not include model-selection uncertainty, recall error, future changes in services or intervention effectiveness. A method ending is not necessarily a lasting exit from modern use.
